Need to revise penalties for animal cruelty 
SHRIMATI PRIYANKA CHATURVEDI (Maharashtra) : Mr. Chairman, Sir, I rise to 
speak for the voiceless animals in our coun try regarding the urgent need to revise 
penalties for animal cruelty.   Sir, there ex ists a stark disparity in our legal system.  A 
person who tortures or kills an animal in Indi a can walk away with a fine of just Rs. 50 
while treating that injured animal costs a minimum of Rs. 5,000 even at Government 
hospital and less said the better about th e Government hospitals because they are 
non-existent literally for the animals in our country.  The perpetrator's penalty is 
literally 100 times less than the victim's tr eatment cost. The Prevention of Cruelty to 
Animals Act, 1960, sets a maximum fine of Rs. 50 for first offence and Rs. 100 for 
subsequent ones.  These amounts, which were fixed over 65 years ago, have failed to 
deter cruelty while animal abuse cases continue  to rise nationwide.   Further, with the 
[ 10 July, 2025 ]
19

 
 
replacement of the Indian Penal Code by the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita (BNS), 2023, 
the earlier offences under Sections 428 and 429 of the IPC are now covered under 
Section 325, which provides for punishment of up to five years imprisonment or fine or 
both. While this is a step forward, enforcement remains weak and penalties under the 
Prevention of Cruelty to Animals Act remain grossly inadequate. 
Sir, a 2021 Report by the Federation of Indian Animal Protection Organisation 
documented that nearly five lakh animal s became victims of crime between 2010 and 
2020. Among these were 2,300 gruesome  intentional acts causing death or 
irreparable harm, including 82 cases of sexu al abuse, 266 cold-b looded killings and 
over 400 violent attacks involving beating, acid-throwing, boiling water and maiming.   
Sir, in the past few months, we have wi tnessed horrifying cas es. In March 2026, 
during Holi celebrations in Haryana, a vira l video showed men forcing alcohol into a 
cow's mouth. There were also reports of  around 100 stray dogs being allegedly 
poisoned to death in Telangana, reported ly on the directions of local Panchayat 
officials in the same month. Yet, India' s National Crime Records Bureau does not 
even include animal cruelty in its annual Cr ime in India Report. We do not formally 
track these crimes, making them invisible to policymaking and enforcement.  Despite 
amendments proposed in 2022 to enhance pe nalties, no meaningful reform has been 
enacted.  Meanwhile, global indices may place India in a moderate category, but 
enforcement remains weak on the ground.  So, through you, Sir, I urge that we 
should relook at the penalties with regard to  the cruelty to animals.  Let them just not 
get away with fines.  Let them be held re sponsible for crimes they do.  In terms of 
cow, even the cattle is abandoned once it…  Sir, I have 20 seconds more. 
MR. CHAIRMAN: I have not said anything. 
SHRIMATI PRIYANKA CHATURVEDI : Sorry, Sir.  If the cattle becomes economically 
of no use, the owners usually abandon them  on the roads, which makes it extremely 
difficult for people to manage , respect or handle them.  So , through you, Sir, I would 
request the Government to look into this issue.  Thank you.

Concern over impact of West Asia conflict on Indian expatriates, seafarers,  
students and LPG supplies 
DR. JOHN BRITTAS (Kerala):   Sir, the whole region, our country, our people, our 
diaspora and, as the Prime Minister said, the whole world is reeling under pressure.  
One crore Indians in the Middle East, 10 per cent of Keralam reside s in Middle East.  
Five lakh Indian students were supposed to take CBSE exams but the exams stand 
cancelled.  There could be a formula for th at.  We are getting substantive remittance 
and India gets the largest remittance, almost Rs.16 lakh crore.  Keralam gets almost 
Rs.2.2 lakh crore.  One-third of Kerala's GSDP is powered by remittance.  I wish that 
there should be a rehabilitation package for all those Gulf returnees and those people 
who are going to return.  The seafarers, 700 of them, are stranded in Strait of 
Hormuz.  I feel that the Government has to make a mechanism to ensure that the 
family members talk to them.  Yesterda y, the Prime Minister addressed the Lok 
Sabha.  There were four major takeaways from his Address.  He talked about 
economic fallout, disruption in supply chain, how it is affecting the daily life of the 
people, the serious situation on the LPG front, and also the condition of the Indian 
diaspora.  What was missing was the silen ce on this unilateral, immoral, illegal war 
that has been unleashed by the United States and Israel. 
Sir, PM called for a unanimous and united voice from the Parliament.  I crave 
your indulgence. You were a Member of Lok Sabha in 2003.  At that time, both the 
Houses of the Parliament, when Atal Bihari Vajpayeeji was the Prime Minister, passed 
a joint resolution, united resolution, condemning the war against Iraq by the United 
States.  I wish that the Indian Parliament , as Prime Minister said, should express 
unanimously a united voice.  Let the Govern ment bring a resolution which should be 
passed by both the Houses.  We term th e attack on GCC countries by Iran as 
egregious, I admit; but what about the genesis of this crisis? I wish that the 
Government does not go by the advice of Shri  Shashi Tharoor who said that silence is 
statecraft.  I wish that they should be guided by the advice from Mallikarjun Kharge ji, 
not from Shri Shashi Tharoor.  I wish that  India being a leader of the non-aligned 
[ 10 July, 2025 ]
27

 
 
nations should feel that silence is not a solution.  We have to make sure that our voice 
is heard and it is not only for the selfish inte rest of the nation but for the interest of the 
larger humanity. So, I call upon the Government to come with a resolution.

Demand for preventive measures to stop milk adulteration in the country 
SHRI YERRAM VENKATA SUBBA REDDY (Andhra Pradesh) :  Thank you, Sir, for 
giving me the opportunity to raise an important issue. My mention is for taking 
preventive measures to stop milk adulteration in the country.  The White Revolution 
made our country from milk deficit nation to world's largest milk producer.  We all 
know, adulteration is normally done when there is shortage of something, not when 
there is abundance.  But, surprisingly and shockingly, in spite of huge production, we 
have rampant adulteration of milk.  Sir, the unfortunate part is, every year, many 
adulteration cases are reported.  A major milk adulteration was busted in Kapaswadi 
32
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
area of Andheri West, Mumbai, in Decembe r 2025.  Recent reports in Punjab show 
that 47 per cent failure rate  for milk and dairy products  tested during 2024-25.  
Authorities busted a factory in Sabarkan tha that had been producing milk and 
buttermilk using detergent and urea.  And, the latest one is reported in Andhra 
Pradesh in February-March, 2026.  Sir, 16 people, mostly elderl y and children, have 
died in Laala Cheruvu, Chowdeswari Nagar in Rajahmundry, Andhra Pradesh, so far 
this month, due to milk adulteration and many  people are still in hospitals.  It is heart-
rending that poor people died due to mi lk adulteration.  There are reports that 
Ethylene Glycol added in milk which perpetually caused death of many people. 
Sir, firstly, I demand compensation of Rs.10 lakhs to the kin of each persons 
who have died due to milk adulteration in  Andhra Pradesh.  An d, secondly, Sir, I 
strongly feel that adulteration can be  controlled and stopped only when FSSAI 
conducts strict and regular surveillance, in spections not superficially but by reaching 
the ground, taking sample of milk and milk products and once find adulterated 
through lab tests, the culprits should be criminally prosecuted and capital punishment 
should be given.  Sir, it is only then we can stop this.  Thank you very much, Sir.

Demand for establishment of Murukku industry cluster at Manapparai in 
Tiruchirappalli 
SHRIMATI RAJATHI (Tamil Nadu): &Honourable Chairman Sir, as Tamil Nadu’s name 
is often identified with Kancheepuram silk , Tiruchirapalli’s name is associated with 
Manapparai   Murukku. Sir,   I would like to draw the attention of the House to the 
world-renowned traditional food industry of Manapparai, Tiruchirappalli. Sir, you also 
would have known about the popularity of  Manapparai Murukku. Its unique taste is 
attributed to the local groundwater and traditional hand-rolled techniques. The 
Manapparai Murukku industry was officially granted the Geographical Indication (GI) 
tag in 2025, recognising its unique taste. A co uple of months ago, the Department for 
                                                           
& English translation of the original speech delivered in Tamil. 
[ 10 July, 2025 ]
33

 
 
Promotion of Industry and Internal Trad e described the Manapparai Murukku as a 
flavourful snack beloved not just in Tam il Nadu but across the world. Despite this 
recognition, thousands of families involv ed in this murukku making industry face 
severe challenges, including absence of sh ared facilities, mode rn infrastructure, 
standardisation mechanisms, and organised marketing systems significantly affecting 
efforts to improve income and livelihood. Th e industry needs support in various forms 
including financial support from the Union Government. 
Sir, I urge the Minsitry to grant approval  for establishing a Murukku Production 
Cooperative Cluster in Manapparai und er the SFURTI (Scheme of Fund for 
Regeneration of Traditional Industries ) programme. This will enable a Common 
Facility Centre (CFC) for quality control, automated production without losing 
tradition, and export-grade packaging. This will not only protect our national heritage, 
but also empower local snacks to reach international markets. 
Moreover, the prices of essential commodities are increasing exorbitantly. 
People face hardship due to skyrocketing of  prices of essential commodities such as 
pulses, edible oil etc. In orde r to relieve them from this fi nancial crisis, their source of

explaining the situation. Sir, I hope my request would be considered. I once again 
thank you for giving me this opportunity. Vanakkam!

Demand to launch National Solar Infrastructure Expansion Mission 
SHRI S. KALYANASUNDARAM (Tamil Nadu): &Honourable Chairman Sir, First of all, 
I convey my greetings to you.  Sir, it is a matter of immense pride for the Tamil soil 
that the high office of the Vi ce President of India is adorne d by you. Your leadership 
brings honour not only to Tamil Nadu but to the entire nation.  L eaders of all political 
parties, esteemed Ministers, dear colleagues, greetings to one and all. 
Thriukural says: 
“He is the light of Kings who has these four things 
Beneficence, Benevolence, Rectitude and care for his people”. 
 
The great leader Kalaignar M. Karunanidhi interpreted this beautifully—he said 
that a true ruler ensures the welfare of the people, governs with compassion and 
fairness, and protects and nurtures society without bias. 
 
Honourable Chairman Sir, today, I rise to draw the attention of this august 
House to a matter of national importance—th e urgent need to expand solar energy 
infrastructure across our country.  India is a land blessed with abundant sunlight 
throughout the year. Yet, paradoxically, we continue to face power shortages and 
electricity instability. The question we must ask ourselves is simple yet profound: In a 
country where the sun rises every day, why should there be darkness? This is not a 
failure of resources—it is a gap in planning, execution, and accessibility. 
Sir, solar energy is no longer an optional initiative—it is a national necessity. 
Despite various schemes, it s benefits have not fully reached the common people, 
particularly in rural and agricultural sectors. We proudly call our farmers the backbone 
of the nation. However, what do they f ace today? Frequent power cuts, rising 
electricity costs, and uncertainty in irriga tion. This situation is neither fair nor 
sustainable.  I, therefore, propose that the Government take a bold and 
transformative step—provide solar-powered pu mp sets free of cost with full subsidy 
to small and marginal farmers, in place of conventional electric pumps. This single 
measure can revolutionize agriculture by  ensuring uninterrupted power supply, 
reducing costs, and increasing productivity. Agriculture will flourish, and farmers will 
progress economically. 
Further, rooftop solar systems must be promoted across the country. For 
economically weaker sections and rural hous eholds, full subsidy sh ould be provided. 
                                                           
& English translation of the original speech delivered in Tamil. 
36
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
For others, the Government can extend supp ort through interest-free or low-interest 
loans. I strongly urge the Government to consider providing up to 75% subsidy for 
solar installations in homes, commercial establishments, and community 
infrastructure. This will significantly reduce pressure on the national power grid. 
Sir, I would also like to propose a visionary national initiative— “National Solar 
Infrastructure Expansion Mission. ” Through this mission, solar energy adoption can be 
systematically expanded across all sectors. If tax incentives and financial concessions 
are extended to industries and commercial establishments adopting solar energy, it 
will not only reduce operational costs but also accelerate economic growth. 
Solarization of Government institutions  must be made mandatory. Hospitals, 
Government schools, colleges, and prima ry health centres should all be equipped 
with solar power systems. This will re duce recurring expenditure and ensure 
uninterrupted essential services. Additiona lly, Corporate Social Responsibility (CSR) 
funds and voluntary organizations can be effectively partnered with the Government to 
support solar expansion projects, particularly in underserved areas. 
Sir, places of worship across our nation can  also play a vital role. Solar panels 
may be installed with full subsidy in such institutions. Those with financial capacity 
may even generate surplus energy, contributi ng to the grid. Moreover, if solar parks 
are developed in every district, it will open new avenues for industrial growth, 
generate employment for our youth, and strengthen the local economy.  A large-scale 
transition to solar energy will also sign ificantly reduce the financial burden on 
Governments—especially in states like Tami l Nadu, where free electricity is provided 
to farmers, weavers, and economically w eaker households. By shifting to solar-
based supply, dependency on costly external power procurement and transmission 
losses can be minimized.  Further, enabling local generation and consumption of 
electricity in low-income residential area s can stimulate local economies and create a 
more sustainable energy ecosystem.  Sir, while  solar energy is crucial, we must also 
promote other renewable sources such as wind and hybrid energy systems through 
dedicated schemes and funding support. 
In conclusion, bringing solar power to every household is not merely a policy 
goal—it is a national commitment. It is our duty to ensure clean, affordable, and 
reliable energy for every citizen of this country. Let us move from intent to action, from 
vision to implementation, and from dependence to sustainability.  With these words, I 
conclude my speech. Thank you, Sir.

Shriman G.C. Chandrashekhar. 
Need to expedite infrastructure and development projects in Karnataka 
SHRI G.C. CHANDRASHEKHAR (Karnataka):  Sir, I thank you for giving me the 
opportunity.  Sir, this is the third time I have got the Zero Hour, but I could not raise it 
since my Zero Hour was at Serial No. 20th or 21st or 22nd.   Today again, my Zero Hour 
submission is at Serial No. 20th, but I got the opportunity to raise it.  Thank you, Sir. 
Sir, I would like to talk about an important issue affecting Karnataka – the 
slowdown in roads, railway and metro pr ojects and how it is directly affecting 
mobility, growth and livelihoods.   This Go vernment keeps talking about rural Bharat.  
But under the PMGSY, Karnat aka received Rs.718 crore in 2022-23.    And, in 2023-
24, the allocation dropped to just Rs.70 crore.   In 2024-25, we got  only Rs.48 crore.   
This is almost a 90 per cent reduction.   So, we can imagine how much importance 
this Government is giving to rural India.   Because of this,   we could construct only 
169 kilometres of roads.  When rural ro ads stop, villages get isolated.  Farmers 
cannot take their produced items to markets.  Even children struggle to reach schools 
and houses.   Sir, roads are not a luxury, but they are the lifelines. 
Coming to National Highways, last week, our honourable Minister was saying 
that we have increased our National Highwa ys network by 50 per cent.   But, Sir, 
Karnataka has been totally ignored.  As compared to 2022-23, they have given almost 
50 per cent less allocation for Karnataka.  There is one more project, which I would 
like to bring to the notice of the House, Sir.    As you know, Sir, Bangalore city is 
facing severe traffic problem.  To control that, a suburban railway project was started 
in 2012, with an allocation of Rs.12,000 cr ore.   As of today, the cost has become 
double but only 19 per cent progress has been  achieved.   They have released just 
Rs.434 crore.    So, you can understand, Sir, how this Government is working.     By 
the time this project is completed, it will cross Rs.45,000 crore or Rs.50,000 crore.   I 
urge the Government, through you, Sir, to kindly look into this and complete it as 
early as possible. 
Coming to railway projects, Sir, the Hassan-Belur railway project costs about 
Rs. 749 crore.  There is zero progress.   The Centre has released only Rs.89 crore.

ORAL ANSWERS TO QUESTIONS 
Changes in BIS certification norms for electronic products 
*331. SHRI ASHOK SINGH : Will the Minister of CONSUMER AFFAIRS, FOOD AND 
PUBLIC DISTRIBUTION be pleased to state: 
(a) whether any changes are proposed or  scheduled to be rolled out in the 
Bureau of IndianStandard (BIS) certifi cation norms with regard to imported 
electronic goods, if so, thedetails thereof; 
[ 10 July, 2025 ]
43

 
 
(b) the present norms for importing el ectronic consumer goods, requirements 
ofcertification and licences; and 
(c) the steps taken or proposed to st rengthen consumer education, product 
safetyawareness and grievance redre ssal mechanisms to ensure better 
protection of consumerinterests across the country? 
THE MINISTER OF CONSUMER A FFAIRS, FOOD AND PUBLIC  
DISTRIBUTION (SHRI PRALHAD JOSHI): (a) to (c) A Statement is laid on the Table 
of the House. 
Statement 
(a)  and (b)  Electronics and Information Technology Goods(Requirement of 
Compulsory Registration Order),2021 has been  notified by Ministry of Electronic and 
Information Technology (MeitY) through wh ich Electronics and IT products have 
been brought under compulsory BIS Registrati on as per provisions of Scheme- II of 
BIS (Conformity Assessment) Regulations, 2018. 
Under this Scheme, a licence to use or apply a Standard Mark, through 
registration based on self-declaration of co nformity, is granted for goods or articles, 
hereinafter referred to as products, ma nufactured in a manufacturing premises and 
conforming to all the requirements of the relevant Indian Standard(s). The 
manufacturer ensures that the product has b een tested for conformity against all the 
requirements of the Indian Standard and subm its test report which is issued by third 
party laboratory. 
At present, 65 product categories have been notified under the schedule of 
“Electronics and Information Technology Goods (Requirement of Compulsory 
Registration) Order, 2021 ”. The Order is equally applicable to imported or 
domestically manufactured goods. 
Earlier, as per Scheme-II of Bureau of Indian Standards (BIS) Conformity 
Assessment (CA) Regulations, 2018, pr ovisions were available for Central 
Government to notify only Standard(s) fo r compulsory BIS certification. Now vide 
Gazette Notification dated 25th February,  2026 in the BIS Conformity Assessment 
Regulations, 2026, Scheme-II has been am ended to include provisions for Central 
Government to notify essential requir ements also besides Standard (s) for 
compulsory BIS certification. 
44
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
Also, under this Scheme-II, the existing  provisions have been amended, so 
that validity of initial Grant of Certificatio n has been increased up  to 5 years (in place 
of earlier up to 2 years) with annual paymen t of fee. Further, the existing provision for 
renewal of Certification up to 5 years with  advance payment of fees for the entire 
renewal duration has been amended to allow annual payment of fee. 
(c) Bureau of Indian Standards (BIS ) organizes awareness programmes and 
other activities on a regular basis through th e network of Regional Offices and Branch 
offices. These programmes includes Awar eness Programme on Standards under 
Compulsory Certification, Hallmarking, Gram Panchayat Sens itization programmes 
etc.  Further, BIS has launched a user-frie ndly mobile app for Android and iOS, 
enhancing consumer access and engageme nt beyond its web portal. The app 
supports 12 languages (10 regional, Hindi, and English) and is free to download. 
The Bureau of Indian Standards (BIS) has established 9,4 37 Standards Clubs 
across the country, including 8,037 in sch ools, as a key initiative to promote 
awareness of quality, safety, and standa rdization among young citizens. Through 
these clubs, students are introduced to th e concept of standards and their role in 
ensuring the safety and reliability of products and services in everyday life. 
BIS has a well-established online comp laints mechanism wherein consumers 
can register through the BIS CARE app or the BIS Complaints Portal 
(https://www.services.bis.gov.in/php/BIS_2.0/). Upon registration, a unique 
complaint number is generated for tracking, and the redre ssal is ensured within the 
prescribed timeline. 
Department of Consumer Affairs also  conducts country-wide multimedia 
consumer awareness campaig ns under the aegis of “JagoGrahakJago” to make the 
consumers aware of Unfair Trade Practices, grievance re dressal mechanisms, other 
important consumer related issues through electronic, digital, outdoor and social 
media and participation in fairs and festivals. 
To reach citizens without smartphones or internet access, the Department 
carried out large-scale IVRS voice call campa igns across the country with a focus on 
Scheduled caste - dominated areas in 2025 in which 23 Cr calls were made. Similarly, 
the Department also conducted large-scal e SMS campaigns in eleven languages in 
which 65 Cr. SMSs were sent to  15 Cr. PD S beneficiaries in first phase and 24 Cr. 
SMSs were sent to consumers in ST domi nated areas, in 2nd phase in 2025. The 
Department also generated awareness by pl aying consumer awareness jingles on All 
[ 10 July, 2025 ]
45

 
 
India Radio during ICC Men's T20 World Cup 2026 matches whic h was broadcasted 
Over 24 Rainbows Stations, 66 Primary Ch annels and 86 Local Radio Stations of 
National and Regional channels of AIR. 
At the grassroots level, the Department conducted weekly virtual sessions with 
Panchayats across various States and Unio n Territories during the year 2024, 2025 
and 2026 wherein more than lakh number of  Panchayats representatives attended the 
sessions. The Department also ran a di gital banner advertisement campaign in 
February 2026 on the IRCTC website and mobile app, generating 78.7 crore 
impressions during the campaign period. 
The National Consumer Helpline (NCH) administered by the Department of 
Consumer Affairs has emerged as a single  point of access to consumers across the 
country for their grievance redressal at a pr e-litigation stage. Consumers can register 
their grievances from all over the country in  17 languages including Hindi, English, 
Kashmiri, Punjabi, Nepali, Gujarati, Mara thi, Kannada, Telugu, Tamil, Malayalam, 
Maithili, Santhali, Bengali, Odia, Assamese  and Manipuri through a toll-free number 
1915. These grievances can be register ed on Integrated Grievance Redressal 
Mechanism (INGRAM), an omni-channel IT enabled central portal, through various 
channels- WhatsApp (8800001915), SMS (8800001915), email (nch-ca@gov.in), 
the NCH app, the web portal (consumerhelpline.gov.in) and the Umang app, as per 
their convenience. 
The “e-Jagriti” portal launched by the Department on 1st January, 2025 to 
enhance consumer grievance redressal, allo ws users to file complaints seamlessly 
from anywhere with multilingual support. 
The Central Consumer Protection Auth ority (CCPA) under the Department 
regulates matters relating to violation of ri ghts of consumers, unfair trade practices 
and false or misleading advertisements wh ich are prejudicial to the interests of 
consumers and to promote, protect and enforce the rights of consumers as a class.

it was the Indian Standards Institution, es tablished in 1947. The ISI became BIS with 
an Act of 1986, and only one product-related Confirmatory Asse ssment Scheme was 
notified. But, in 2015, considering the increased significance of the Standards, -- our 
Prime Minister, Shri Narendra Modi ji, want s quality products; he always keeps telling

a reality even in India, that is, ‘zero effe ct and zero defect’ -- this has been done. 
Now we have converted the BIS into a national  standard body of India. It is totally

This is the vast expansion which has happe ned in the last 11 years under our Prime

one for process and system and service. Th ese are the important things which have 
been done in the last few years. 
Now, as far as the MSME is concer ned, besides hand-holding, minimum 
marking fees payable has been reduced for 80 per cent of micro-scale and start-up 
units, 50 per cent fee exemption for the sm all-scale industries, 20 per cent for the 
medium-scale units, and additional concession  of 10 per cent is also provided to the 
women MSME and units of any scale loca ted in the North-Eastern States. These 
concessions have been further extended up to 31 st May, 2029. Apart from that, many 
training camps, online training programmes and hand-holding initiatives are being 
run. Regarding the exact numbers for Ma dhya Pradesh where licences have been 
issued, I will directly send the details to th e honourable member as I do not have the 
exact number at present.

SHRI PRALHAD JOSHI: Sir, as it is, whether a product is imported or manufactured in 
India, the system of issuing licence remains the same. For electronic products 
[ 10 July, 2025 ]
47

 
 
certified by BIS, several measures have b een undertaken to improve Ease of Doing 
Business and reduce the compliance burden. The online CRS Portal has completely 
digitised the certification operation, prov iding an end-to-end solution. Manufacturers 
can log-in with their credentials, submit documents and access their own dashboards 
to respond to BIS from their convenient location.  The renewal certification process 
has also been automated by doing away with human intervention. Manufacturers only 
need to submit details and pay the fee and  the  endorsement  is generated 
automatically through the system with no human interference. As far as MSMEs are 
concerned, I have already explained this and do not wish to repeat it.  For electronic 
products notified by the line Ministry, we create standards as per the request of the 
concerned Ministry. Accordingly, for electron ic products notified by MeitY under the 
BIS Act, obtaining certifi cation licence from BIS is compulsory. Without this 
certification and presence of the standard mark, notified electronic goods cannot be 
sold in the Indian market.  Another important point is that the Compulsory 
Registration Order (CRO) is in place for elec tronic goods in the Indian market. This 
order covers around 65 products notified  by the Ministry of Electronics and 
Information Technology. I would like to inform the House, through you, that all 
standards and QCOs are decided by the concerned Ministry. We develop these 
standards in consultation with the concerned Ministry and experts.  Recently, in some 
cases, factory visits have also been mini mised and are conducted only once in five 
years to ascertain quality. Market samples are drawn and if a sample fails, BIS issues 
a notice. In such cases, we inform the Ministry of Electronics and Information 
Technology which takes further action. Our role is limited.   If  the  product  passes,  
there is no issue; if it fails, we inform th e concerned Ministry, particularly, the Ministry 
of Electronics and Information Technology.

SHRI PRALHAD JOSHI: Sir, I appreciate the concern of the entire House as far as this 
issue of quality is concerned.  As far as guidelines on misleadin g advertisements are 
concerned, these are issued by the Centra l Consumer Protection Authority (CCPA).  
Under the Legal Metrology Act, we have  six compulsory declarations on every 
packing.  Apart from that, with regard to sugar or any other content in the product, 
the norms regarding what has to be disp layed are normally decided by the FSSAI, 
which is under the Ministry of Health.  Our organizations and FSSAI are regularly 
interacting.  Whenever we come across an y issue of misleading advertisement, not 
only relating to such packages but also relating to other things, we take action.  As far 
as misleading advertisements are concerned, many people have been fined.  As far 
as packaging is concerned, we are continuously interacting with the FSSAI.  The 
information which is displayed on the packs is  also as per the Legal Metrology Act.  If 
this is not done, appropriate action is taken.  This process will continue to be there.

Healthcare facilities in North Eastern States 
*332.  SHRI BIRENDRA PRASAD BAISHYA: Will the Minister of HEALTH AND FAMILY 
WELFARE be pleased to state: 
(a) whether Government has reviewed the availability of healthcare facilities in 
NorthEastern States; 
(b) the details of funds sanctioned for improving hospitals, medical colleges    
andtelemedicine services in the region; and 
(c)  the steps taken to deve lop more hospitals, specifically in rural areas of North 
Easternregion? 
THE MINISTER OF STATE IN THE MI NISTRY OF HEALTH AND FAMILY  
WELFARE (SHRI PRATAPRAO JADHAV): (a) to (c) A Statement is laid on the Table 
of the House. 
Statement 
(a) to (c)  The Ministry of Health an d Family Welfare (MoHFW), Government 
of India regularly reviews the availability of healthcare facilities across the country 
including for the North Eastern States. Under the National Health Mission (NHM), the 
[ 10 July, 2025 ]
51

 
 
review is carried out mainly through Common Review Mission (CRM) which is 
conducted annually to review the implentation status of NHM. 
Further, monitoring and evaluation of th e progress of all States/ UTs is done 
through review meetings, video conferences an d field visits of senior officials. Key 
deliverables of the States/UTs  including North Eastern States are also reviewed in 
National Programme Coordination Committee (NPCC) meetings. 
MoHFW is implementing the following programs on Mission mode to enhance 
medical facilities across the country including North Eastern States: 
(i)  NHM  - Under NHM, MoHFW provides n eed-based technical and financial 
support of the States/UTs to strengthen  the public healthcar e system including 
telemedicine, based on the requirements  posed by States/UTs in their annual 
Programme Implementation Plans (PIPs) as  per their health needs, and within 
the overall resource envelope in the Reco rd of Proceedings (RoP). The details of 
RoP approvals given to the States includ ing NE States may be accessed from the 
following link: 
https://nhm.gov.in/index1.php?lang=1&level=1&sublinkid=1377&lid=744 
(ii)  Pradhan Mantri Ayushman Bharat H ealth Infrastructure Mission (PM-
ABHIM): PM-ABHIM is a Centrally Sponsored Scheme (CSS) with additional 
Central Sector components designed to support primary, secondary and tertiary 
healthcare facilities. It also focuses on enhancing the healthcare system's 
capacity to respond effectively during emergencies. 
Under the CSS component of the sche me, support is provided to North 
Eastern States for strengthening of 10 83 Building-less Sub Health Centre- 
AyushmanArogyaMandir (SHC – AAM) with an amount of Rs. 574.41 Cr, 4 Urban- 
AAMs with an amount of Rs.2.85 Cr, 207 Bloc k Public Health Units (BPHUs) with an 
amount of Rs.182.28 Cr, 111 Integrated Public Health Labs (IPHLs) with an amount of 
Rs. 201.79 Cr, and 37 Critical Care Blocks (CCBs) in District Hospitals with an 
amount of Rs.908.68 Cr. 
Central release under NHM for North East ern States from FY 2021-22 to FY 
2025-26 is given at Annexure-I. 
The state wise details of units and budg et approved in North Eastern States 
under PM-ABHIM is given at Annexure-II.

[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
Apart from this, the Ministry of Health  and Family Welfare administers a CSS 
for ‘Establishment of new medical colleges attached with existing district/referral 
hospitals’ with preference to underserved areas and aspirational districts, where there 
is  no  existing  Government  or  private  medical  college.  Under  the  Scheme, all the  
 
envisaged 157 Government medical co lleges have already been approved 
including 12 medical colleges in North East Region. 
In addition to above, Pradhan Mant riSwasthya Suraksha Yojana (PMSSY) 
aims to correct regional imbalances in the availability of affordab le/reliable tertiary 
healthcare services and also to  augment facilities for qualit y medical education in the 
country, including North Eastern States. Under the PMSSY, All India Institute of 
Medical Sciences (AIIMS) has been set up  at Guwahati.  Further the details of 
Government Medical Colleges/Institutions (GMCIs) approved for upgradation under 
PMSSY in North Eastern States is attached at Annexure-III. 
In addition, Central government institut ions like North Eastern Indira Gandhi 
Regional Institute of Health and Medical Sciences (NEIGRIHMS) at Shillong, Regional 
Institute of Paramedical and Nursing Scie nces (RIPANS) at Aizwal and Regional 
Institute of Medical Sciences (RIMS) at Imphal provide quality healthcare in North 
Eastern States.

Portability benefits under ONORC Scheme 
#*333. SHRI SHAMBHU SHARAN PATEL : Will the Minister of CONSUMER AFFAIRS, 
FOOD AND PUBLIC DISTRIBUTION be pleased to State: 
(a) the current number of beneficiaries receiving portability benefits under One 
Nation One Ration Card (ONORC) Scheme; 
(b) whether Government has assessed th e migration trends reflected through 
ONORC transactions; 
(c) if so, the details thereof;  
(d) the number of inter-State portability transactions recorded during the last two 
years;and 
(e) the steps taken to ensure the uninterru pted availability of ration for migrant 
workers? 
THE MINISTER OF STATE IN THE MINISTRY OF CONSUMER AFFAIRS, FOOD AND 
PUBLIC DISTRIBUTION (SHRIMATI NIMUBEN JAYANTIBHAI BAMBHANIYA): (a) to 
(e) A statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
Statement 
(a) to (e) The One Nation One Ra tion Card (ONORC) scheme is a 
technology-driven initiative of the Departme n t  o f  F o o d  a n d  P u b l i c  D i s t r i b u t i o n  t o  
                                                           
# Original notice of the question was received in Hindi. 
[ 10 July, 2025 ]
57

 
 
enable nationwide portability of ration cards for beneficiaries covered under the 
National Food Security Act (NFSA). 
The ONORC facility has been implemented in all 36 States/Union Territories, 
covering more than 80 crore beneficiarie s under the Pradhan Mantri Garib Kalyan 
Anna Yojana (PMGKAY). Under this system , beneficiaries can lift their entitled 
foodgrains from any Fair Price Shop (F PS) across the country using their existing 
ration card with Aadhaar-bas ed biometric authentication on  electronic Point of Sale 
(ePoS) devices. 
The system particularly benefits migran t workers and other mobile populations 
by ensuring seamless access to subsidized food grains irrespective of their location. It 
operates through integration of ration card databases of all States/UTs and 
interoperable ePoS devices at FPSs. 
The State-wise portability data captu ring instances where beneficiaries 
registered in one State (home State) lift th eir entitled foodgrains in another State 
(sale State) are available on public doma in i.e Integrated Management of Public 
Distribution System (IMPDS) portal (www.impds.nic.in).

recorded during the last two years is placed at Annexure.

Shortage of RIG and ARV injections 
#*334. DR. BHIM SINGH : Will the Minister of HEALTH AND FAMILY WELFARE be 
pleased to state: 
(a)  whether the production of Rabies  Immunoglobulin (RIG) and Anti-Rabies 
Vaccine (ARV)injections for the treatment  of Rabies in the country is less 
than the requirement, yet theyare being exported; and 
(b)    if so, the details of their production and export in the last three years? 
THE MINISTER OF HEALTH AND FAMI LY WELFARE (SHRI JAGAT PRAKASH 
NADDA): (a) and (b) A Statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
Statement 
(a) and (b) Health is a State subject, the responsibility of strengthening public 
healthcare system lies with the respective State/ UT Governments. Under National 
Health Mission (NHM), the Government prov ides technical and financial support to 
States/UTs for strengthening their healthcare systems based on the proposals 
submitted by them in their Program Impl ementation Plans, subject to norms and 
availability of resources. 
Under the National Rabies Control Programme (NRCP) funds have been 
allocated through NHM to States/UTs under National Free Drug Initiative (NFDI) for 
                                                           
# Original notice of the question was received in Hindi. 
[ 10 July, 2025 ]
63

 
 
purchase of Rabies Immunoglobulin (RIG) and Anti-Rabies Vaccine (ARV). 
Procurement of RIG and ARV is decentralized. 
Advisories are being issued from time to time to all States/UTs to maintain 
uninterrupted supply of   ARV and RIG through funds allocated under NFDI. 
India has an installed capacity for produc tion of approximately 81.7 million vials 
of ARV and 17.7 million vials of RIG a nnually. Year-wise consolidated data on 
production of Anti-Rabies Vaccine (ARV) and Rabies Immunoglobulin (RIG) is not 
centrally maintained as manufacturing is undertaken by multiple private and public 
sector entities.

Directorate General of Commercial Intelligence and Statistics, export in the last three 
years of RIG and ARV are as under:

MR. CHAIRMAN:  Now, Q. No. 335.
 
Availability of essential medicines in Government hospitals 
 
#*335.  SHRI BRIJ LAL : Will the Minister of  CHEMICALS AND FERTILIZERS  be 
pleased to state. 
 
                                                           
# Original notice of the question was received in Hindi. 
[ 10 July, 2025 ]
67

 
 
(a)  whether Government has assessed the availability of essential medicines in 
Government   hospitals; 
(b)   if so, the details thereof; 
(c)  the steps taken to encourage the ava ilability of affordable medicines through 
the Pradhan Mantri Bhartiya Janaushadhi Pariyojana (PMBJP); 
(d)  the number of JanaushadhiKendras currently operational across the country; 
(e)  whether there is a proposal to in clude additional categories of medicines 
under the scheme; and 
(f)   if so, the details thereof? 
 
THE MINISTER OF CHEMICALS AND FERTILIZERS (SHRI JAGAT PRAKASH 
NADDA): (a) to (f) A statement is laid on the Table of the House. 
 
Statement 
 
(a) to (f) As per information provided by  Ministry of Health and Family Welfare 
(MoH and FW) to ensure availability of essential drugs and reduce the Out-of-Pocket 
Expenditure (OOPE) of the patients visiting  the public health facilities, Government 
has rolled out the Free Drugs Service Initiative (FDSI) under National Health Mission 
(NHM). Under this, financial support is pr ovided to States / Union Territories for 
provision of free essential medicines in public health facilities based on the 
requirements posed by them in their Prog ramme Implementation Plans (PIPs) within 
their overall resource envelope. 
 
MoH and FW has recommended facility wi se Essential Medicines List (EML) 
including drugs for cancer treatment to be  made available at the public healthcare 
facilities: 
 
Sub Health Centre Health   Wellness Centre (SHC-HWCs) - 106 
Primary Health Centre Health and Wellness Centre (PHC-HWCs) - 172 
Community Health Centres (CHCs) - 300 
Sub-District Hospital - 318 
       District Hospital (DHs) – 381 
 
Support under the scheme is availa ble for Procurement of drugs and 
strengthening /setting up robust systems of procurement, Quality Assurance, Supply 
chain management and warehousing, Pres cription audit, gr ievance redressal, 
dissemination of Standard Treatment Guidelines and Establishment of IT enabled 
platform DVDMS (Drugs and Vaccine Distribution Management System) for 
68
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
monitoring the real status of procuremen t and availability of essential medicines.To 
facilitate the monitoring of supply chain management activity at National level, a 
Central Dashboard has been de veloped. Few states have implemented the roll out of 
the DVDMS portal till Sub health centres wh ile many others are attempting to do so 
for the successful monitoring of the real status of procurement and availability of 
essential medicines. 
Furthermore, under the Pradhan Mantri Bhartiya Janaushadhi Pariyojana of 
Department of Pharmaceutical, Ministry of Ch emical and  Fertilizera total of 18,646 
Jan Aushadhi Kendras (JAKs) have been opened across the country as on 
28.02.2026,out of which 2,370, Jan Aushadhi Kendras has been openedspecifically in 
Government hospitalsto provide medicine s at rates that ar e about 50% to 80% 
cheaper than those of branded medicines. 
The product basket under the Pradhan Mantri Bhartiya Janaushadhi Pariyojna 
has 2,110 medicines and 315 surgicals, me dical consumables and devices, covering 
all major therapeutic groups, such as cardiovascular, anti-cancer, anti-diabetic, anti-
infectives, anti-allergic and gastro-intestina l medicines and nutraceuticals. Almost all 
generic medicines included in the National  List of Essential Medicines, except lab 
reagents and vaccines, are part of the product basket. 
With a view to ensure accessibility and availability of medicines at JAKs, the 
following measures are in place: 
(i)     An end-to-end information-technology-enabled supply chain system 
consisting currently of five wareho uses and 41 distributors across the 
country, is in place. 
(ii) Since September 2024, stocking by Jan Aushadhi Kendras (JAKs) of 200 
commonly used medicines, consisting of the 100 top-selling medicines in 
the product basket and 100 fast-se lling medicines in the market, is 
incentivised with JAK owners being eligible to receive monthly incentive 
based on the stocks that they maintain of these medicines. 
(iii) In addition, with a view to ensure availability of commonly used products, 
400 fast-moving products are monitored regularly by the scheme 
implementing agency {Pharmaceutical s and Medical Devices Bureau of 
India (PMBI)} and demand for the same is forecasted on an ongoing basis. 
Further, steps have been taken to di gitise the forecasting method to 
augment the procurement process through automation. 
[ 10 July, 2025 ]
69

 
 
The product basket has been reviewed from time to time by a committee to 
evaluate the market demand of existing and newly launched/off-patent medicines, 
based on feedback and analysis from JAKs, citizens and market reports.

MR. DEPUTY CHAIRMAN: Mananiya Shrimati S. Phangnon Konyak. 
SHRIMATI S. PHANGNON KONYAK (Nagaland) : Honourable Deputy Chairman, Sir, 
I rise to support the Appropriation (No.2) Bill, 2026--a legislation that authorizes 
withdrawal of approximately Rs. 53.5 lakh crore from the Consolidated Fund of India 
for the Financial Year 2026-27. This Bill is no t merely a financial instrument; it is a 
solemn commitment to the people of India,  especially women, youth, marginalized 
communities and also to the North-Eastern Region.  Under the visionary leadership of 
our honourable Prime Minister, Shri Narendra Modi, and the able  stewardship of our 
honourable Finance Minister, Shrimati Nirmala Sitharaman, this Appropriation Bill 
transforms the Union Budget 2026-27 into actionable reality. 
This Bill has been passed after rigor ous parliamentary procedures. The 
Demands for Grants were guillotined in the Lok Sabha and later returned by the Rajya 
Sabha after detailed deliberations. This Bill also empowers the Government to 
allocate resources to national priorities an d promote inclusive growth and sustainable 
development. Public Capital Expenditure has reached a historic high of Rs. 12.20 lakh 
crore, which is more than four times the le vel recorded in 2017-18. This demonstrates 
the Government's unwavering  commitment to building a Viksit Bharat by 2047. The 
creation of an Economic Stabilization Fund with an allocation of Rs. 57,381 crore 
further strengthens India's resilience agai nst global economic uncertainties. What 
makes this Appropriation Bill truly transformati ve is its strong focus on social equity 
and inclusive welfare. It aims to uplift women, empower youth, eradicate poverty, 
support marginalized communities and ac celerate the development of the North-
Eastern region. I would like to elaborate on these areas. 
Sir, women are the backbone of our nati on's progress. The gender Budget for 
2026-27 has been enhanced to Rs.5.01 lakh crore marking an increase of over 11 per 
cent compared to the previous year. This allocation forms nearly 9.4 per cent of the 
total Union Budget and stands as the hi ghest-ever gender-focu sed expe
nditure in 
India's history. Under women-specific sche mes, flagship programmes such as the 
86
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
Pradhan Mantri Awas Yojana (Gramin and Urban), Deen Daya l Antyodaya Yojana, 
National Rural Livelihood Mission and ‘Missi on Shakti’ have received a significant 
financial support. These initiatives aim to  provide housing, security, financial 
independence, clean energy access and social protection to millions of women. A 
revolutionary initiative introd uced in this Budget is th e establishment of Self-Help 
Entrepreneur Marts (SHE-Marts). These community-owned retail outlets will enable 
lakhs of women-led self-help groups to ev olve from micro-financing networks into 
full-fledged entrepreneurial enterprises. Over the next five years, term loans of up to 
Rs.2 lakh crore will be extended to five lakh women, especially, from Scheduled 
Castes and Scheduled Tribes to encour age first-time entrepreneurship. The 
allocation of the National Ru ral Livelihood Mission has b een significantly enhanced, 
directly benefiting more than ten crore rural women. Working women's hostels are 
also being established across multiple dist ricts to ensure safe accommodation and 
dignified living conditions for working women. In addition, the Government has 
proposed to establish one girls’ hostel in  every district within higher education 
institutions focused on science, technology , engineering and mathematics. This will 
greatly encourage female participation in ad vanced technical fields. Special emphasis 
is also placed on strengthening the care economy. Nearly 1.5 la kh caregivers will be 
professionally trained in healthcare, wellness and assistive technologies, creating 
dignified employment opportunities while improving care services for the elderly and 
differently-abled. Nutrition and welfare schemes for women and children have also 
been strengthened. Food fortification programs under the su pplementary nutrition 
scheme and direct benefit transfers under gi rl child welfare schemes will significantly 
improve maternal and child health ou tcomes. These measures are not mere 
allocations, they are instruments of tr ansformation. They promote financial 
independence, safety, health security and di gnity for every woman. This Bill ensures 
that the dream of Lakhpati Didi becomes a reality and guarantees that funds meant for 
women empowerment are utilized efficiently. 
India's youth represents the driving for ce of our nation's future. The Budget 
2026-27 is proudly youth-centric. Recogniz ing their aspirations and potential, 
significant allocations have been made for skill development, employment generation 
and education reforms. The Pradhan Mantri Kaushal Vikas Yoja na has received a 
substantial boost to expand skill training programs across diverse sectors. Emerging 
fields such as animation, visual effect s, gaming and comics are being promoted 
through these specialized content creator la bs in thousands of schools and colleges. 
This initiative aims to prepare millions of  young professionals for the global digital

economy. Special skill development programmes have also been designed for the 
divyangjan enabling them to secure em ployment in high-demand sectors like

commitment to inclusive development. High er education infrastructure is also 
receiving strong support. University town ships are being developed near industrial 
corridors to promote industry and academia collaborations. Artificial intelligence-
driven learning platforms will equip stud ents with future-ready technological skills. 
The Government has also introduced majo r employment initiatives under National 
Development Missions. Large-scale pub lic and private sector employment 
opportunities will be generated promoting entrepreneurship and innovation among 
youth. These initiatives aim to transform job-seekers into job creators. When we pass 
this Appropriation Bill, we invest in the dreams of millions of young Indians.  From 
rural villages to metropolitan cities, yo uth will gain access to quality education, 
modern skills and dignified employment opportunities.

seen a major increase, reflecting the Government's commitment to strengthening 
rural infrastructure and livelihoods. The Pr adhan Mantri Garib Kalyan Anna Yojana 
continues to provide free foodgrains to vulnerable households, ensuring food security 
for millions.  Employment guarantee schemes continue to serve as lifelines for rural

received increased funding to ensure di gnified living standards for economically 
weaker sections.  The agricultural sector has also been prioritized with strong financial 
support for farmers, particularly small an d marginal landholders.  Investments in 
irrigation, growth diversification, modern  technology and agri-entrepreneurship will 
boost rural incomes and productivity.  A sa turation approach has been adopted to 
ensure that every eligible beneficiary recei ves full welfare support, whether in the form 
of Ration Cards, housing assistance, pensions or skill training.  This guarantees that 
no deserving citizen is left behind.  Th is Bill is not merely about Government 
expenditure; it represents a promise to uplift the most vulnerable sections of society 
and ensure equitable development.  In adva ncing social justice, India's Constitution 
envisions equality and protection for Sche duled Castes, Scheduled Tribes, Other

Dedicated development programs for tribal communities continue to receive strong 
support, particularly for vulnerable tribal groups living in remote areas.  Educational 
scholarship for Scheduled Castes and Sche duled Tribes students will continue to 
promote access to higher education.  Assi stive technology and rehabilitation services 
for Divyangjan are being expanded.  Modern  centers for assistive devices will improve 
mobility, independence and quality of life  for the differently-abled citizens.  
Entrepreneurship programs for women belonging to Scheduled Castes and 
Scheduled Tribes and minority communities will promote financial inclusion and self-
reliance. Through this Bill, the Government strengthens its resolve to deliver social 
justice and ensure equal opportunities to all sections of society.  There is a special 
focus on the North-Eastern region as the gateway to India's growth. The North-
Eastern region is an integral part of India' s cultural and economic fabric.  Recognizing 
its strategic and developmental importance,  the Government has made substantial 
allocations for the region's growth.  Infr astructure projects focusing on roads, 
connectivity, power supply, water resour ces and tourism are being accelerated.  
Special schemes aim to promote local indu stries, eco-tourism and skill development 
among the youth.  Healthcare and educationa l institutions in the region are being 
strengthened to provide better services.  Vocational training institutions and programs 
tailored to local needs will enhance the employment opportunities. Agriculture and 
allied sectors such as horticulture, bamboo industry, agarwood and high value crops 
are being promoted to increase rural pros perity.  Enhanced connectivity through 
railway, highways and digital infrastructure will integrate the region more closely with 
the national markets.  These initiatives ensu re that the North-East transforms from a 
peripheral region into a powerful engine of India's development growth.  Sir, the 
Appropriation (No. 2) Bill 2026 is far more than  a financial statement.  It is a blueprint 
for inclusive national transformation.  It empowers women with dignity and 
independence, equips youth with modern skills and employment opportunities, uplifts 
the poor through welfare support, promotes justice for marginalized communities and 
accelerates development in the North-Eastern region.  With a historic total 
expenditure and record capital investment, this Bill lays a strong foundation for a self-
reliant, equitable and developed India.  I urge this House to pass the Bill unanimously 
and reaffirm our collective commitment to na tional progress and social justice. Thank 
you, Sir.

poor women in Andhra Pradesh

to poor women during 2025-26 to clear pend ing applications.   PMUY is providing 
LPG connections to adult women from poor  households and, so far, 10.33 crore 
connections are given.  This has been a ppreciated by women, who otherwise suffer 
by burning solid fuels like wood, dung an d crop residues.  Women prefer LPG gas 
since it lowers indoor air pollution leading to better respiratory health. 
Secondly, everybody is appreciating that th e Government of India is giving    
Rs. 300 subsidy per cylinder for 9 refills to ease financial burden on poor women. 9.72 
lakh connections are given to poor wome n in Andhra Pradesh under PM-Ujjwala for 
which they are grateful to honourable Prime Minister.  Out of these, 1.74 lakh belongs 
to SC/ST communities.  But, there are st ill 60,000 applications pending from poor 
women for LPG connections from AP.  All a pplicants are eagerly waiting that they 
would get connection out of 25 lakh approv ed.  Hence, I request Government to 
consider all 60,000 applications from An dhra Pradesh and prov ide poor women LPG 
connections at the earliest.  I also request for increasing the LPG subsidy from        
Rs. 300 now being given per cylinder to Rs . 500 which will go a long way in easing 
financial burden on women. Thank you.

Shri Harsh Vardhan Shringla. 
Concern over operational and maintenance challenges faced by the               
UNESCO heritage, Darjeeling Himalayan Railway 
SHRI HARSH VARDHAN SHRINGLA (Nominated) :  Madam, I seek to draw attention 
to the need to preserve the Darjeeling Himalayan Railway, popularly known as the Toy 
Train, a UNESCO World Heritage Site under the Mountain Railways of India. Built in 
1881, this engineering marvel connects New Jalpaiguri to Darjeeling across fragile 
Himalayan terrain.  For over 140 years, it ha s symbolized the region’s cultural identity 
and attracted tourists from India and abroad. However, serious operational and 
maintenance challenges now threaten its survival. Despite efforts by the Ministry of 
Railways, the railway corridor has gradua lly shrunk, compromising safety and 
efficiency. The line passes through land slide prone areas wh ere heavy rainfall 
frequently damages tracks and disrupts services, requiring constant monitoring and 
restoration. Ageing steam locomotives also demand specialised maintenance and 
custom built parts, necessitating sustained technical and financial support. The vision 
of “Vikaas bhi Viraasat bhi ” rightly emphasizes balancing development with heritage 
conservation. This Railway embodies that vision must be preserved through 
sustainable tourism and infrastructure strengthening. There is a need to enhance 
visibility through strategic marketing and to  develop an integrated tourism ecosystem 
linked with local culture and livelihoods. Ac tive participation of local communities, 
tourism stakeholders, and Self Help Groups can generate employment. Skill 
development of youth and railway staff in heritage conservation and modern 
maintenance is equally essential. 
I urge the Government to formulate a comprehensive conservation plan with 
adequate funding, infrastructure restor ation, corridor protection, and disaster 
mitigation measures. Preserving this railway  is preserving a living heritage and the 
identity of the hills.

Need for social campaign to address underage driving 
SHRI LAHAR SINGH SIROYA (Karnataka) : Madam, I seek to bring attention of the 
Government to the growing concern around underage driving in In dian cities. I would 
like to illustrate this issue with two incident s that took place in and around the city of 
Bengaluru last month, that is February 2026.  First, in an accident that took place on 
14th February 2026, in Hoskote Taluk, just ou tside Bengaluru, seven lives were lost 
and, out of them, five were minors. In this accident, a teenager was driving an SUV at 
150 kmph. Two days later, on 16 th February, on Tumkur Road, in the outskirts of 
Bengaluru, five youngsters in a car were  killed, among whom three were minors. 
[ 10 July, 2025 ]
157

 
 
Again, in the driving seat was a teenager. Cases related to teenage driving has risen 
consistently in Bengaluru city in the last two years. The police have booked around 
414 cases in 2025. This is not the story of Bengaluru city alone, as there are similar 
media reports from Delhi, Pune, Nagpur , Chennai, Hyderabad, Visakhapatnam 
among other cities. Minors who get behind the wheels don’t just put their lives in 
danger but endanger others on the streets too. There are enough  rules and fines but 
they have not stopped undera ge drivers from seeking their thrill. Parental oversight 
alone is not enough.  I, therefore, sincer ely urge the Government and the Ministry of 
Road Transport and Highways to develop and undertake an effective multimedia 
social campaign on underage driving. Thank you, Madam.

Shri Masthan Rao Yadav Beedha. 
Demand for Integrated Buckingham Canal–based multimodal corridor in Andhra 
Pradesh under NW-4 with key focus on SPS Nellore district 
SHRI MASTHAN RAO YADAV BEEDHA (Andhra Pradesh) : Vice-Chairman Madam, I 
draw attention of the Government to the ur gent need for integrated development of 
the Buckingham Canal corridor in Andhra Prad esh, particularly in Sri Potti Sriramulu 
Nellore District, under National Waterway-4 (NW-4). This calls for coordinated action 
by the Ministry of Ports, Shipping and Waterways, Inland Waterways Authority of India 
(IWAI), and convergence with the Sa garmala and Bharatmala programmes. 
Buckingham Canal traverses about 257–258 km  in Andhra Pradesh, and the Nellore 
stretches are less encroached and technically  feasible. Official alignment studies and 
KML-based mapping demarcate a 60-metr e waterway corridor and a 30-metre 
parallel road corridor, safeguarding canal boundaries while enabling planned 
infrastructure development. This allows simultaneous canal restoration and road 
development, unlike congested urban stre tches elsewhere on NW-4. The Nellore 
corridor provides multimodal connectivit y between Krishnapatnam Port and the 
proposed Ramayapatnam Port, linking industrial hubs and hinterland areas. A 
combined road-and-waterway corridor can reduce logistics costs, decongest 
158
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

 
 
highways and improve industrial efficiency. It  also offers scope fo r five to six future 
industrial cities, generating employment and balanced regional growth. The Union 
Budget 2026–27 reiterates emphasis on in land waterways, port-led development, 
multimodal logistics, and coastal infrastr ucture. The Buckingham Canal corridor is a 
natural candidate for convergence.   I urge upon the Government to sanction a 
Detailed Project Report (DPR), take up the Nellore stretch as a pilot corridor, and 
establish a Centre–State mechanism with IWAI for time-bound execution.

Dr. Sasmit Patra. 
Concern on missing Indian Seafarers in International Waters 
DR. SASMIT PATRA (Odisha): Madam, I seek to highligh t the case of a young cadet 
from Odisha, who has gone missing in in ternational waters under unexplained 
circumstances. At the very beginning of his career, he boarde d a commercial vessel 
with aspirations of supporting his family and building a future. Instead, his sudden 
disappearance, after he was last seen on board has left his family in prolonged 
uncertainty, with no clear answers despite search efforts and limited transparency 
from authorities. This is not an isolated  incident. Official records reveal numerous 
Indian seafarers reported missing or dead under unclear conditions, with many cases 
still pending investigation years later. Thes e include incidents of explosions, onboard 
accidents, disappearances, and even alleged negligence by ship authorities. In one 
case, a cadet went missing mid-voyage  despite multiple onboard searches, 
highlighting serious gaps in monitori ng and accountability. Further, official 
communication indicates that nearly 50 Indian seafarers have died in a single foreign 
region since 2014, many of them between 18 and 24 years old. Several cases also 
point towards mental harassment, unsafe working environments, and delays in 
compensation and investigation. I, therefore, demand time-bound international probe 
into seafarers’ disappearance, mandatory r eal-time tracking and reporting of Indian 
crew on all vessels, a National Maritime Missing Persons Authority and a time-bound 
accountability for all pending cases.

Need to ensure pay parity and appropriate remuneration for judges in India 
SHRI KARTIKEYA SHARMA (Haryana) :  Sir, I draw the attention of Government the 
need to review and ensure pay parity an d appropriate remuneration for judges in 
India. The judiciary is a cornerstone of our constitutional democracy. Judges carry the 
profound responsibility of safeguarding th e Constitution, protecting citizens’ rights, 
160
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

and upholding the rule of law. However,  despite the critical nature of this 
responsibility, judges in India receive sign ificantly lower remuneration compared to 
their counterparts in many other major de mocracies. This disparity becomes even 
more concerning when we consider the dema nding nature of judicial work, the high 
standards of integrity expected from judges, and the need to attract the most capable 
members of the Bar to the Bench. Compet itive and dignified remuneration is not 
merely a matter of compensation; it is di rectly linked to judicial independence, 
institutional strength, and the quality of ju stice delivery. In recent years, concerns 
have also been raised about lack of pari ty between judicial remuneration and the 
compensation structures of other high co nstitutional and senior public offices. 
Addressing this gap is essent ial to preserve the dignity of  the institution and ensure 
that the judiciary continues to attract the fine st legal minds.   I,  therefore, urge the 
Government to undertake a comprehens ive review of the salary and service 
conditions of judges, keeping in mind inte rnational benchmarks and the principle of 
institutional parity.  Strengthening the condit ions of service of judges is ultimately an 
investment in the credibility, independen ce, and effectiveness of India’s justice 
system.
